# Chapter 1: The Cryptocurrency Revolution

# Understanding The Basics Of Cryptocurrency

Electronic virtual currencies originated way before the inception of the first-ever scalable crypto - Bitcoin. However, these currencies weren’t termed cryptos because they do not have the cryptography tech embedded in them. But these are the very first attempts that eventually led to the invention of the currently used cryptocurrencies.   

The revolution of digital currencies started in the Netherlands in the 1980s.  A group of developers invented an electronic card system to avoid the thefts in Gas Stations that happen at night. Whoever wants to access Gas can use those cards, so the use of real money is eliminated.

The second attempt towards virtual currencies is made by ‘David Chaum.’ He is an American cryptographer and the inventor of Blinded Cash. The USP of this currency is that it is untraceable. That is, transactions made using this virtual currency cannot be tracked. Chaum used complex encryption techniques to achieve this feat. However, this crypto has failed because of various scalability issues.    

We must know that eCash, B-Money, Bit Gold, and Hashcash are notable failed attempts in this space. eCash was the first conceptualized digital token currency to transfer between individuals. B Money was portrayed as an electronic cash system that is anonymous and secured at the same time. Bit Gold is considered the first digital asset with complete decentralized functionality. Bit Gold was pegged to the value of gold while moving away from centralization.

HashCash was one of the most successful digital currencies, getting everything in place except for scalability. It functions almost like Bitcoin and has a similar consensus algorithm too. This digital currency was truly ahead of its time, which could be one reason for its failure. Finally, in 2008, the whitepaper of Bitcoin was released, while in early 2009, the first ever Bitcoin block was mined, and the rest is history.

Cryptocurrency is a kind of digital money that exists only online. There is only an actual coin or bill if you utilize a service that enables you to exchange cryptocurrencies for physical tokens. Typically, you trade cryptocurrencies with another person online, through your phone or computer, without a third party's assistance, like a bank. Cryptocurrencies are unrelated to valuable things – they are irrelevant to anything of actual worth in the real world, which causes their value to fluctuate wildly, as you have probably seen.

In contrast to equities, bonds, artwork, real estate, or precious metals, cryptocurrencies have no intrinsic value or use. For instance, gold is a critical component of electronics and jewelry — and therefore has worth in addition to its scarcity. On the other hand, cryptocurrency costs money solely because someone else owns it and needs money to deliver it to you.

Cryptocurrencies address the following critical issues associated with the use of cash. There is a need for a centralized authority to control these objects' value, manufacturing, and authenticity. Possibility of fraudulent duplicating. To understand this better, one should get a good understanding of some of these basics.

# Cryptography

Cryptocurrency uses cryptography — the art of concealing and disclosing information — to guarantee the security of user data and the integrity of transactions. As the name implies, a cryptocurrency is digital or virtual money protected by encryption, making it hard to counterfeit or double-spend.

# Blockchain

A blockchain is a subset of Distributed Ledger Technology (DLT), a distributed database distributed among many operators (nodes, computing devices, etc.) Numerous cryptocurrencies are decentralized networks powered by blockchain technology—a distributed ledger maintained by a distributed network of computers.

This is the underlying technology that drives cryptocurrency. It is a digital ledger that validates transactions, accounts, and balances. Blockchain may be used for various applications outside financial transactions, including supply chain management, monitoring art ownership, and even digital collectibles.

A node is a word associated with blockchain technology that will be used throughout this essay. A node is a discrete component of a larger data structure known as a blockchain. Without nodes, the system would implode. Cryptography and blockchain technology assist cryptocurrencies in the creation of new coins, the enforcement of legal transactions, and the establishment of a secure system.

# Decentralized

A distinguishing characteristic of cryptocurrencies is that they are not issued by any central authority, which supposedly protects them against government intervention or manipulation. As shown by Bitcoin, decentralization implies that all authoritative source is spread evenly across all peers on a network, and there is no single point of failure. For example, to "hack" Bitcoin, someone must access at least 51% of the vast network of computers that operate Bitcoin, which is considered impossible.

# Peer-to-Peer

Without a broker, cryptocurrency may be transferred directly between two individuals. These transactions are made with minimal processing costs that benefit the network, allowing customers to avoid the high transaction fees associated with more conventional money transfer services.

There are many cryptocurrencies in existence. You've almost certainly heard of some of the most prominent cryptocurrencies, like Bitcoin, Litecoin, and Ethereum. Cryptocurrencies are becoming an increasingly popular alternative to traditional online payment methods. Before you convert actual dollars, euros, pounds, or other conventional currencies to cryptocurrencies, it's essential to understand what they are, the dangers associated with their use, and how to safeguard your investment.

A cryptocurrency is a digital money that was developed using encryption techniques. It is a type of alternative payment. Due to the usage of encryption technology, cryptocurrencies serve as both a medium of exchange and a virtual accounting system. A cryptocurrency wallet is required to utilize cryptocurrencies. These wallets may be cloud-based services or software installed on your PC or mobile device. Wallets are the devices that hold the encryption keys that verify your identity and connect you to your Bitcoin.

Cryptocurrencies are still in their initial stages, and their market is very volatile. As banks or any other third party does not regulate these, they are often uninsured and challenging to convert into real money (such as US dollars or euros.) Additionally, since cryptocurrencies are intangible technology assets, they may be hacked like any other. Finally, since you keep your cryptocurrencies in a digital wallet, you will lose your entire cryptocurrency investment if you lose access to your wallet.

# Properties Of Cryptos

In the middle of the 2008 Financial Crisis, an unknown person or group of people known as Satoshi Nakamoto published a whitepaper titled "Bitcoin: A Peer-to-Peer Electronic Cash System." At the time, very few individuals were aware of this game-changing breakthrough in money.

Cryptocurrency has become an alternate trade means due to the development of encryption and blockchain technology. Since its inception in 2009, Bitcoin has grown in popularity across the globe. It has garnered so much attention as a result of its unique features.

To properly comprehend the cryptocurrency revolution, it's critical to understand the basic features of cryptocurrencies and blockchain technology that contribute to its revolutionary and game-changing status. While there are many cryptocurrencies in the modern world, each with its distinct features, cryptocurrency, at its core, has three essential traits.

# Trustless

Bitcoin is trustless because it was built to require no one to trust anybody else to operate. Before Bitcoin, every form of money needed you to charge a central authority to use it. In each of these instances, the foremost authority becomes the primary weakness that ultimately results in the currency's downfall. With Bitcoin, each ecosystem component verifies what the others are saying without relying on anybody. When a Bitcoin transaction is broadcast, all nodes receive it and verify that the signatures are genuine. If the signatures are invalid, the transaction is discarded.

Because everyone on the network has a copy of the ledger, we no longer need to rely on a single entity, organization, or third party, as there is no need to trust when you can check against this ledger using your copy. The blockchain is the decentralized ledger, and Individual network players being rewarded through a proof-of-work (PoW) consensus mechanism is one of the most revolutionary concepts in contemporary economics.

The incentive may assist nodes in maintaining their integrity. If a greedy attacker can build more CPU proof-of-workers than all the honest nodes, he must choose between defrauding people by reclaiming their money and generating new currencies.

He should consider it more lucrative to follow the rules, which reward him with more new coins than everyone else combined, than to undermine the system and the legitimacy of his riches. While we are still discovering how and why we utilize cryptocurrencies, they are here to stay. Apart from the other advantages of cryptocurrencies and blockchain technology, resolving the centralized trust problem alone is a significant breakthrough that will ensure the long-term viability of crypto.

# Unchangeable/Immutable

In the most straightforward meaning, "immutable" implies "unable to be undone." Immutability should be based on three concepts about blockchain and cryptocurrency: Rewriting history should be extremely unlikely or difficult. Anyone other than the owner of a private key should be unable to transfer money. The blockchain records all transactions to ensure the two principles above.

We consult our bank's transaction history to see how our money has been spent from our bank accounts. We entrust our banks with the responsibility of not fabricating transactions or manipulating our money, just as we entrust them with the responsibility of delivering our transactions to receivers. If fraudulent transactions occur, the bank must also be trusted to reverse them and rectify the problem.

As previously shown, a cryptocurrency needs more centralization and trust; there is no longer a third party in whom we can place our confidence to perform these functions. As a result, transaction records are made publicly accessible and irreversible (immutable). Although changing the transaction ledger is not impossible, cryptographic security makes it very difficult. It necessitates the compromising of the whole Bitcoin network.

# Distributed

Because "decentralization" is such a hot topic in the crypto world, it's critical to describe it precisely. It may have a variety of connotations "Blockchains are decentralized politically (no one controls them) and architecturally (there is no single point of failure in the infrastructure), but they are centralized conceptually (there is a single generally accepted state, and the system acts like a single computer)." — Vitalik Buterin.

Fault Tolerance: since decentralized systems rely on networks of independent components, they are less likely to fail unintentionally.

Attack Resistance: Because decentralized systems lack susceptible central points that may be attacked at a far lower cost than the surrounding system, they are more costly to attack, destroy, or control.

Collusion Resistance: individuals of decentralized systems find it more difficult to behave in ways that benefit themselves at the cost of others. On the other hand, businesses and governments constantly cooperate in ways that enrich them while harming others.

Besides central banks and governments, banks regulate the supply and production of money via mints and interest rates. Users of fiat currencies are at the whims of central banks regarding money creation.

# Scarcity and Limited Supply

Fiat currencies have an infinite supply because central banks can issue and print as many as they wish. Central banks often influence the value of their nations' money as part of their economic strategies. The inflationary nature of fiat currencies implies a gradual decline in the currency's value over time. On the other hand, most cryptocurrencies have a finite and predetermined supply programmed into their underlying algorithm when they are established.

However, with cryptocurrencies, a person or consortia can only control or exercise substantial influence over the money supply with the majority's consent. The most popular cryptocurrencies have limited tokens or an endless pool with pre-defined manufacturing criteria. Like Bitcoin, Litecoin, and Dash, numerous popular other cryptocurrencies have a finite supply, making them naturally deflationary. Any rise in demand or acceptance of the cryptocurrency will result in a price increase.

# How Can Cryptos Change The Way We Transact?

To further comprehend this, let us examine the cryptographic transaction process. Assume Julia wants to give Luke $25 in BTC. Luke gives Julia his Bitcoin address ("public key"). This Bitcoin address is associated with any Bitcoin exchange or wallet Luke creates on the Bitcoin Blockchain. It appears as follows: 3GTDmd3JohJ4oDbTnvgw68gEDS3y6CS7Wx.

Julia puts that address and the Bitcoin (BTC) amount — about 0.0009 BTC, equal to a little under $25 — into his cryptocurrency exchange or wallet and clicks send.

Luke gets the BTC less a transaction fee that the BTC miners and the network charge. These costs may vary from $0.05 for an hourly delivery to $0.58 for a ten-minute delivery. It makes no difference whether Julia sends $25 or $25,000,000 - the costs would remain the same. Now, let us dissect what occurs behind the scenes. From the time Julia sends the transaction to the blockchain, every node in the Bitcoin network receives the transaction request. Each node verifies that Julia is really whom they claim to be. The nodes verify Julia's identification using the private key – a private key uniquely identifies the source of the funds being sent. Whoever has access to this small substantial gains access to your funds? This is why it is critical to safeguard your private key. And we always want to use our wallets and not keep the coins on exchanges, more about that later, but remember Your Keys, Your Crypto.

Because the nodes have a copy of the transactions' entire ledger, they can verify that Julia has the funds. If at least 51% of nodes agree on the two components mentioned earlier, the transaction is valid, and the nodes update the ledger with the new marketing.

If we compare this to a typical transaction involving fiat money, we can see how complex crypto transactions are. These additional layers contribute something to the table and may address a weakness in conventional financial systems. Here are a few ways that cryptocurrencies have the potential to revolutionize the way we do business.

# Transactions That are Faster, Cheaper, and More Secure Than Banks

Today's banking system needs to be updated. International bank transfers may take up to a week since both require correspondent banks and country-specific clearing institutions. Even cross-border exchange of payment data is fraught with difficulties and frictions. Bank transactions may be done quickly and inexpensively using a digital currency like Bitcoin.

Fraud is a significant issue when it comes to virtually everything involving money. Whether it's a money transfer or just using a credit card on an unsecured website, the potential for fraud is a significant worry for everyone. This is particularly true for individuals who have previously been a victim of fraud.

Because cryptocurrency is not linked to your bank account or cash and is transferred electronically and securely through the blockchain, which records all transactions, cryptocurrency will significantly decrease fraud and provide peace of mind.

# Global Remittances Get a Boost.

Each year, migrants from poor nations send over $500 billion in remittances home, surpassing foreign direct investment. With international transfer costs averaging 6-10 percent of the entire amount sent, the impact on some of the world's most disadvantaged individuals is significant. Technology offers the ability to accelerate and reduce the cost of these transfers.

Private users may even transfer money straight to their relatives through cell phones using virtual currency, with the remaining costs coming from currency exchanges. While conventional money transfer businesses must maintain capital to compensate for delays in international money movement, enterprises with digital currencies have considerably lower capital needs. Capital carrying expenses and money transfer charges are just a portion of the cost of doing business for remittance companies. Reduced costs, however, may make it simpler for smaller companies to join and create new remittance corridors or for current players to service smaller cities or new nations.

# Money That is Secure for the Impoverished

The inflation and volatility suffered by certain nations' conventional currencies are alarming. While not all nations' currencies are susceptible to exorbitant inflation rates, some may significantly gain from adopting Bitcoin.

For instance, the growth of mobile technology in Africa has already shown that poor nations can be technological leaders. According to estimates, up to 60% or more of trade in Kenya occurs via mobile phone credits as a means of exchange. Anyone who has a mobile phone may use it to store money and transfer credits to another user. The issue is that the costs are high: traditionally, cashing out has cost up to 20%, even though many customers may use credit cards directly without paying significant fees.

Digital currencies have the potential to become another simple and secure method of payment in nations where the majority of people lack bank accounts. While adopting Bitcoin as a secondary currency in a nation exposes people to currency risk, it may be preferable to current alternatives, especially in countries with severe inflation. For instance, it would be more secure physically than keeping cash at home or purchasing gold jewelry. Additionally, a Bitcoin owner may swap it for a more stable currency on one of the worldwide Bitcoin exchanges. This may broaden access to worldwide financial markets, allowing even the unbanked to save and safeguard against inflation. One possible consequence is that capital restrictions become more challenging to implement.

# Unleashing the E-Commerce Potential

Concerns about credit card theft are causing several internet businesses to turn away potential customers today. Such fraud is particularly prevalent in overseas transactions, so many companies refuse to take foreign payments. It cannot be reversed once a transfer is performed using a digital currency like Bitcoin. This reduces merchants' risk of fraud and enables them to sell globally. Additionally, since virtual currencies enable consumers to transfer money as simply as email, online purchasing would become a far more pleasant experience.

Additionally, digital money may enable small companies in underdeveloped nations to participate more fully in global e-commerce. Latin American artisans might sell their wares worldwide, Chinese adolescents could provide Mandarin instruction through Skype, and African businesses promoting their products on online advertising markets would have a payment option that is now absent. Small-value transactions are a compelling use case since minimal transaction costs allow low-value in-app purchases or micropayments to read online news items from media sources worldwide.

# Money That Can Be Programmed With Smart Contracts

Once a digital asset is created, it may be transferred automatically. This opens the door for implementing "programmable money" and "smart contracts." Escrow accounts are a realistic example. These accounts are already utilized in significant transactions, like real estate purchases. The buyer deposits funds in escrow, and is released to the seller only when the seller transfers the title to the property. This method may be utilized for even smaller amounts in the digital era when trust can be a significant barrier to people transacting at arm's length.

Another example is multi-sig, which requires several people to authenticate before money can be withdrawn from an account. This may be used to prevent the theft of digital currencies. Still, it could also guarantee that money is not "lost" or stolen when it travels across borders, between business divisions, or between charity groups and contractors in poor nations.

Additionally, programmable money may be used in far more sophisticated contracts, such as financial transactions with many parties and complex derivatives. You might invest some money in a financial agreement that pays out based on the performance of specific stock values. A computer program might be connected to the Bloomberg terminal feed, and then, based on the performance of certain stocks or combinations of equities, various people may get money.

Can Cryptos Replace The Centralized Financial System?  Before we address this issue, we must consider how centralized financial systems came to be. This would enable us to assess the function of central financial institutions and determine if cryptos might eventually replace them.

# Central Banks: Their History

In 1844, the English developed central banking with the Bank Charter Act, a legislative initiative that established the foundation for an organization with exclusive authority over currency issuance. The notion was that a bank with so much power might contribute to the financial system's stability during times of crisis. It's a notion that many experts believe played a role in averting catastrophe during the 2007-2008 financial crisis and the subsequent Great Recession.

Over time, central banks have developed. For example, the Federal Reserve utilizes monetary policy to Attain full employment and price stability. Assure the nation's banking and financial systems are secure and sound and allow customers to get credit. In times of crisis, stabilize the financial system. Contribute to the oversight of the nation's payment systems. The Federal Reserve and other central banks may adjust interest rates and create or destroy money to accomplish these goals.

For instance, if the economy seems to be expanding too fast and prices for goods and services become unaffordable, a central bank may raise interest rates to make borrowing more costly. Additionally, a central bank may deplete the economy of money by decreasing the quantity of money it makes accessible to other banks for lending reasons. Because most funds reside on computerized balance sheets, just pressing delete may cause it to vanish. Thus, the quantity of money available to buy products is reduced, presumably resulting in a decrease in pricing.

Naturally, every action has a corresponding response. While demonetization may result in lower costs, it makes it more difficult for companies to borrow money. As a result, these companies may become more cautious, hesitant to spend, and reluctant to recruit new employees.

Central banks may lower interest rates or create money if an economy needs to develop more rapidly. Reduced interest rates make borrowing money less costly and more accessible and attractive to businesses and individuals. Similarly, central banks may expand the funds available to banks for lending.

Additionally, central banks may attempt to influence economies in other ways. These measures may involve the open market purchase of securities (bonds) to create demand. High demand results in reduced interest rates since borrowers no longer need to provide a higher rate because the central bank offers a willing buyer.

Attempts by central banks to guide economies toward prosperity are loaded with danger. Inflation may become a concern if interest rates are kept too low. The economy may stall when prices rise and people need help to afford their desired goods. When interest rates are too high, borrowing is restricted, and the economy is harmed.

Low-interest rates (compared to other countries) encourage investors to withdraw money from one country and transfer it to another, providing a better return rate. Consider the predicament of retirees whose income is dependent on rising interest rates. When interest rates are low, their buying power and capacity to pay their expenses are directly impacted. Sending money to a nation that provides a higher rate of return is a sensible move.

Interest rate and money supply manipulation also directly impacts the value of a nation's currency. A high currency makes exporting products more costly for local businesses. This has the potential to result in domestic unemployment. A depreciating dollar raises imported products' prices, such as oil and other commodities. This may increase the cost of imports for consumers and domestic producers of products that depend on imported components or materials. A weak dollar benefits a slowing economy needing a boost, while a strong dollar benefits consumers.

Because there is a lag between when a central bank initiates a policy adjustment and when that move affects a nation's economy, central banks are always looking forward. They aim to implement policy changes now that will allow them to accomplish future objectives.

Now let's look at the other side of the coin. The inherent complexity of national and global economies provides ammunition for the claim that these economies are too unpredictable to be effectively controlled via the manipulation practiced by central banks. This argument, advanced by adherents of the Austrian School of Economics, may justify the adoption of a peer-to-peer currency like Bitcoin that does away with central banks and their intricate systems.

Since their creation, modern central banks have been a source of contention. And the causes for dissatisfaction are many and diverse. On the one hand, the idea of monopolistic power is very unsettling to many individuals. On the other hand, the presence of an autonomous, opaque organization that can influence an economy is much more alarming.

Numerous individuals (including economists and politicians) think that central banks make errors that significantly affecting citizens' lives. Among these errors are the following: Increases in the quantity of money (creating inflation and hurting consumers by raising prices for the goods and services they purchase).

Interest rate hikes are being implemented (hurting consumers who wish to borrow money). The development of measures that maintain an abnormally low rate of inflation (resulting in unemployment). Abnormally low-interest rates (creating asset bubbles in real estate, stocks, or bonds)

In this vein, no less an authority than former Federal Reserve Chair Ben Bernanke blames the Great Depression of 1929 on central bank manipulation (which increased interest rates).

In an era where technology enables consumers to conduct trade without the intervention of a central authority, central banks are obsolete. This argument is strengthened by a more comprehensive study of the financial sector.

The Great Recession and a slew of scandals were precipitated by banking sector corruption. Bankers have wreaked havoc in Greece and other countries. Several organizations, like the International Monetary Fund, have been accused of prioritizing profits above people. And on a more local level, bankers earn billions of dollars by acting as intermediaries in individual transactions. In this climate, many individuals find abolishing the financial system attractive.

To sum up, at the moment, central banks are the primary mechanism through which countries control their economies and look at every war fought this century.  Every battle was in a country with no central bank until the war ended. They have monopolistic power and are unlikely to relinquish it without a struggle. While Bitcoin and other digital currencies have attracted considerable curiosity, their adoption rates are low, and official backing is practically nonexistent. It makes sense when you realize that banks are not just for transactional purposes, as most cryptos are. They have a far broader set of goals than cryptos. All of these make sense on paper, but when the Federal Reserve is no more Federal than Federal Express, and it was created by the Bank of England and the Rothschilds to print money for the financial elite anytime they want, you can imagine they will do everything that can to keep this system in place for their benefit.

Governments and the puppets that run them get out of the control of the financial elite and accept Bitcoin or any other cryptocurrency as a legal currency; it stands little chance of ever completely replacing central banks. Central banks the world over are monitoring and analyzing Bitcoin. Given the high expense of manufacturing metal coins (which often exceed their face value), it is more probable than not that central banks will issue their own Central Bank Digital Currency (CBDC). CBDCs have become the latest trend among central bankers worldwide. The coalition of advanced economies is making strides toward introducing CBDCs globally. At a recent meeting in Washington, Japanese Finance Vice Minister Masato Kanda announced that the G7 has made it a top priority to explore ways to assist developing countries in implementing CBDC by appropriate standards, including the G7 public policy principle for retail CBDC.

CBDCs are the government's response to the rise of cryptocurrencies. They seamlessly blend the innovative technology behind Bitcoin with the tried-and-true monetary policies of fiat currencies such as the dollar, euro, and renminbi. Unlike traditional digital currencies such as bitcoin or even dollar-pegged stablecoins like TetherUSDT or USDC, CBDCs offer governments complete visibility and control over every economic transaction.

Indulge in the deliciousness of a New York City street taco without a worry in the world. Your privacy is paramount as there is no government record of your identity or purchase when you hand over a $5 bill to the street vendor. Experience the revolutionary difference of CBDCs, the complete antithesis of conventional paper currency. Introducing the e-CNY, China's revolutionary CBDC that provides the government real-time insights into consumer behavior. With the ability to track purchases and locations, the e-CNY allows the government to regulate transactions deemed unfit for society. Need medicine, the government will know. Have an addiction that costs money, the government will know everything.

With the ability to replace private-sector checking and savings accounts with government accounts, CBDCs offer a new level of convenience, security, and no civil liberties or privacy. Additionally, they provide the Federal Reserve unlimited ability to print and allocate money, just like today with the paper dollar. CBDCs even allow for the subtraction of money from individuals' bank accounts, making them a powerful tool for governments to manage their economies and control whom they deem wrong. Think about it: with their CBDCs, they can make and enforce their own rules in the digital space. If you want to use your digital currency, let's hope you have an excellent social score and have been a good citizen.

And why wouldn’t they? Think about it: with their CBDCs, they can make and enforce their own rules in the digital space. If you want to use your digital currency, let's hope you have an excellent social score and have not been a bad citizen.
